---
number: 26.5
title: 'The Asynchronous Kernel: Tensor Cores & TMA'
description: 'The modern kernel is a factory: dedicated hardware moves the tiles, dedicated hardware multiplies them, and the threads that remain are managers. WGMMA, TMA, swizzling, and the pipeline.'
part: 'The Machine'
status: 'ready'
---

## The idea in one sentence

The fastest matrix multiply on a modern GPU is a factory: one piece of hardware fetches the tiles,
another multiplies them, and the threads that are left do nothing but say when.

## The kernel that stopped being the best one

Lecture 26 climbed to 93.7% of NVIDIA's own library, and every rung of that ladder asked one thread
to do three jobs at once. Work out an address. Fetch the numbers sitting at it. Multiply them. The
last rung got 64 results into a single thread's registers and kept the arithmetic units fed by hand,
with a rhythm of loads and barriers the programmer wrote out line by line.

Look at what that kernel does not use. It never touches a tensor core, even though the card it runs
on has them. Every multiply-add is issued by a thread, for a thread, on numbers that thread loaded.
That is the shape of a fast kernel from before the hardware changed underneath it.

The hardware then took two of the three jobs away. Fetching a tile became the work of a dedicated
copy engine that one thread can order and then forget about. Multiplying a tile became a single
instruction that 128 threads issue together and none of them owns the result of. What is left for
the threads is the third job, saying when, and this chapter is about why that turned out to be the
hard one.

The worklog behind the numbers here is Pranjal Shankhdhar's{' '}
<a href="https://cudaforfun.substack.com/p/outperforming-cublas-on-h100-a-worklog">H100 worklog</a>,
which walks eleven kernels from 32 to 764 TFLOP/s on one H100. The explanation that pulled the
machinery into one place is Aleksa Gordic's{' '}
<a href="https://aleksagordic.com/blog/matmul">Inside NVIDIA GPUs</a>. Both are worth reading after
this chapter, and both are credited where their numbers appear.

<Callout type="note">
  No CUDA in this chapter ran on the machine it was written on, which has no NVIDIA GPU and could
  not execute a single asynchronous instruction below even if it had one. Every listing is a
  skeleton for reading, cut to the lines that carry the idea. Every measured number is somebody
  else's, named and linked where it appears. The arithmetic between the numbers is ours, and it was
  done here.
</Callout>

## The ceiling moved

Start where Lecture 26 started, with what a perfect kernel would cost, because the answer is no
longer the same answer.

Peak arithmetic on a GPU is one multiplication of three numbers. How fast the clock ticks, how many
tensor cores are listening to it, and how many operations one tensor core finishes in one tick.
Nothing else enters, which is why the result is called a speed of light: it is what you would get if no thread
ever waited for anything.

Check the multiplication on a chip whose answer is published. An A100 has 108 multiprocessors with 4
tensor cores each and a 1.41 GHz boost clock, and NVIDIA rates it at 312 TFLOP/s of dense
half-precision arithmetic. Divide and you learn what one tensor core does in one tick:

$$
\frac{3.12 \times 10^{14}}{108 \times 4 \times 1.41 \times 10^{9}} \;=\; 512 .
$$

Five hundred and twelve floating-point operations per core per clock, which is a round number
because the hardware was built to a round number.

Now do the H100. It has 132 multiprocessors, still 4 tensor cores each, so 528 in all. NVIDIA's{' '}
<a href="https://developer.nvidia.com/blog/nvidia-hopper-architecture-in-depth/">Hopper architecture post</a>
{' '}says the fourth-generation tensor cores deliver "2x the MMA computational rates of the A100 SM
on equivalent data types", which turns 512 into 1024. Horace He's post on matmul timing gives the
chip's top clock as 1.830 GHz. Multiply:

$$
528 \times 1024 \times 1.830 \times 10^{9} \;=\; 9.894 \times 10^{14} .
$$

</svg>
  The speed of light of one H100, multiplied out. Yellow are the three numbers you look up, green is
  the product. NVIDIA's specification table prints 1,979 TFLOP/s for this chip because it counts
  structured sparsity, which doubles the rate; half of that number is the one above.
</Figure>

989 TFLOP/s. Lecture 26's whole ladder lived under a 30 TFLOP/s roof.

The other half of the roofline moved much less. An H100 SXM reads its own memory at 3.35 TB/s.
Divide one by the other and you get the arithmetic intensity a kernel must reach before arithmetic,
rather than memory, is what limits it. With the ordinary FP32 units, which NVIDIA rates at 67
TFLOP/s on this card, that ridge point sits at 20 floating-point operations per byte. With the
tensor cores it sits at 295. Same chip, same memory, and a bar almost fifteen times higher.

Now price a tile against that bar. A block tile of $m$ rows by $n$ columns marching along $k$ reads
$m + n$ numbers for every step of $k$ and does $2mn$ operations on them. In bf16 a number is two
bytes, so over the full depth $K$ the intensity is

$$
I \;=\; \frac{2mnK}{2K(m+n)} \;=\; \frac{mn}{m+n},
$$

which is Lecture 26's formula with the byte count halved. The largest tile in this chapter is
$128 \times 256$, and it gives $I = 32768/384 = 85.3$. That is nowhere near 295. On paper the tile
alone caps you at $85.3 \times 3.35 = 286$ TFLOP/s, which is 29% of the machine.

  <svg slot="drawing" viewBox="0 0 532 344.25" width="532" role="img" aria-label="A horizontal strip of arithmetic intensity, pink on the left where memory decides and green on the right where arithmetic decides. Dashed marks above put the plain FP32 ridge at 20 FLOP per byte and the tensor core ridge at 295. A dot at 85 marks a 128 by 256 tile, and a blue arrow carries it to 502 once 83 percent of its bytes are served by the L2 cache." style="max-width:100%;height:auto;min-width:540px">

<text x="0" y="10.572" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="12px" fill="var(--color-accent)" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">83% of those bytes never leave the chip,</text>
</g>
<g transform="translate(212.82999999999998 285) rotate(0 98.965 7.5)" data-s="20">
<text x="0" y="10.572" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="12px" fill="var(--color-accent)" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">so DRAM sees one in six: 502</text>
</g>
<g transform="translate(123.1 318) rotate(0 157.185 8.125)" data-s="21">
<text x="0" y="11.453" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="13px" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">FLOP per byte that actually reaches DRAM</text>
</g>
</svg>
  One chip, two ridge points. The tensor cores move the bar for being compute bound from 20 FLOP per
  byte to 295. A 128 by 256 tile asks for 85, deep inside the memory-bound half. What carries it
  across is the L2 cache: Pranjal Shankhdhar measures an 83% hit rate for his kernel, which leaves
  DRAM one byte in six.
</Figure>

That gap is why this chapter has a second half. Getting near the speed of light is not a question of
picking a bigger tile. It is a question of arranging for most of the bytes a tile asks for to be
found on the chip already, and that turns out to be a question about the order the tiles are visited
in.

<Callout type="warning">
  A speed of light is not a constant. Horace He's{' '}
  <a href="https://www.thonking.ai/p/strangely-matrix-multiplications">investigation of matmul timing</a>{' '}found
  that the same multiply runs measurably faster on all-zero inputs than on random ones, because a
  transistor that does not flip costs no power, and the card is running into its power limit before
  its arithmetic limit. His conclusion is the one to keep: matmuls on an H100 "are primarily not
  compute or bandwidth limited, they are power limited". The 989 above assumes a clock the chip
  cannot always hold.
</Callout>

## The tile is the instruction

Here is the change that undoes Lecture 26's mental model.

On Hopper the matrix multiply instruction is called `wgmma`, for warp-group matrix multiply
accumulate. A warp-group is four warps, so 128 threads, and they issue it together. For bf16
operands its shapes are written `m64nNk16` with $N$ running from 8 to 256 in steps of 8, which means
the instruction consumes a $64 \times 16$ block of $A$ and a $16 \times N$ block of $B$ and adds
their product into a $64 \times N$ accumulator.

Take $N = 64$ and count. That one instruction performs $64 \times 64 \times 16 = 65{,}536$
multiply-adds, which is 131,072 floating-point operations. Lecture 26's inner loop did two of them
per lane per issue.

<Figure n="3">
  <svg slot="drawing" viewBox="0 0 538.38 311.85" width="538" role="img" aria-label="On the left three small orange boxes labelled a, b and c forming one multiply-add worth 2 FLOP. A blue arrow labelled 65,536 of those points right to a matrix multiply drawn as an orange 64 by 16 block of A above an orange 16 by 64 block of B feeding a green 64 by 64 block D, labelled 131,072 FLOP in one instruction." style="max-width:100%;height:auto;min-width:520px">

<g transform="translate(294.98 257.6) rotate(0 24.004999999999995 7.500000000000014)" data-s="21">
<text x="0" y="10.572" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="12px" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">64 × 16</text>
</g>
<g transform="translate(477.6 85.6) rotate(0 5.199999999999989 8.125)" data-s="22">
<text x="0" y="11.453" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="13px" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">B</text>
</g>
<g transform="translate(477.6 103.6) rotate(0 24.004999999999995 7.5)" data-s="23">
<text x="0" y="10.572" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="12px" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">16 × 64</text>
</g>
<g transform="translate(402.95 170.8) rotate(0 6.435000000000002 9.375)" data-s="24">
<text x="0" y="13.215" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="15px" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">D</text>
</g>
<g transform="translate(382.76 192.8) rotate(0 28.64500000000001 8.125)" data-s="25">
<text x="0" y="11.453" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="13px" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">64 × 64</text>
</g>
<g transform="translate(335.7 10) rotate(0 80.41 8.75)" data-s="26">
<text x="0" y="12.334" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="14px" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">one wgmma.m64n64k16</text>
</g>
<g transform="translate(327.21 30) rotate(0 89.75500000000002 7.5)" data-s="27">
<text x="0" y="10.572" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="12px" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">issued once by 128 threads</text>
</g>
<g transform="translate(309.15 285.6) rotate(0 109.61500000000001 8.125)" data-s="28">
<text x="0" y="11.453" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="13px" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">131,072 FLOP in one instruction</text>
</g>
</svg>
  The unit of work, before and after. On the left is what one lane issued in Lecture 26: one
  multiply and one add, on three numbers that lane owns. On the right is one wgmma of shape
  m64n64k16, which eats a 64 by 16 block of $A$ and a 16 by 64 block of $B$ whole. The same
  arithmetic would take 65,536 of the instruction on the left.
</Figure>

Two operands and a result, and none of them is where you would expect. Operand $B$ must be in shared
memory. Operand $A$ may be in shared memory or in registers. And the accumulator, the thing you
actually wanted, is spread across the whole warp-group.

That last one is the sentence that ends the thread as a unit of thought. A $64 \times 64$
accumulator holds 4096 floats. A warp-group has 128 threads. So each thread holds $4096/128 = 32$ of
them, and the declaration in a real kernel is a plain array, `float d[N/16][8]`, which at $N = 64$ is
`d[4][8]`, which is 32. No thread can read the answer. No thread can print it. Asking one thread
what the product is has stopped being a question with an answer.

<Figure n="4">
  <svg slot="drawing" viewBox="0 0 665.63 309" width="666" role="img" aria-label="A green 64 by 64 grid labelled the answer tile, 4096 numbers, beside four rows of 32 small boxes standing for the 128 threads of a warp-group, with one box yellow and marked as holding 32 registers. Underneath, 128 times 32 equals 4096 registers, and a short table giving 8, 32 and 128 registers a thread at instruction widths 16, 64 and 256." style="max-width:100%;height:auto;min-width:560px">

</g>
<g transform="translate(212 284) rotate(0 115.23500000000001 7.5)" data-s="159">
<text x="0" y="10.572" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="12px" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">128, against a thread limit of 255</text>
</g>
</svg>
  The accumulator of one wgmma, and the registers that hold it. Every one of the 4096 numbers in the
  tile lives in exactly one of the 128 threads, 32 apiece, and the count comes out even at every
  width the instruction offers.
</Figure>

The widths matter for a reason a programmer meets fast. At $N = 256$ the accumulator is
$64 \times 256 = 16{,}384$ floats, so 128 registers per thread, and a CUDA thread may hold at most
255 registers in all. One warp-group therefore cannot own a $128 \times 256$ output tile: two
instructions of that width would want 256 accumulator registers per thread, more than the whole
budget, with nothing left over for addresses or operands. That single constraint is why the fast
kernels in this chapter run two consumer warp-groups rather than one, each owning half the tile.

The instruction is also asynchronous, which is where this chapter gets its title. It returns before
the answer exists. Around it sit three more instructions whose only job is to say when the
accumulator may be touched.

```cpp
// one warp-group, one accumulator, three fences around the multiply
float d[WGMMA_N / 16][8];                     // 32 registers when N = 64

asm volatile("wgmma.fence.sync.aligned;");    // freeze d: the hardware owns it
wgmma_async(d, desc_a, desc_b);               // wgmma.mma_async...m64n64k16
asm volatile("wgmma.commit_group.sync.aligned;");   // close the batch
asm volatile("wgmma.wait_group.sync.aligned 0;");   // only here does d hold it
```

The two descriptors are not pointers. They are packed words that tell the tensor core where the
operand sits in shared memory, how far apart its rows are, and how it was scrambled on the way in,
which is the subject two sections down.

<Callout type="intuition">
  In Lecture 26 a thread was a worker: it owned a rectangle of the answer, and everything it did was
  in service of that rectangle. From wgmma onward a thread is a shareholder. It holds a scattered 32
  of the 4096 numbers and can do nothing useful with its share alone. The unit that owns a rectangle
  is the warp-group, and the unit that computes is a piece of silicon no thread can see inside.
</Callout>

## One thread orders the groceries

Now the other job that left. Getting a tile of $A$ from global memory into shared memory used to be
a loop: every thread worked out an address, issued a load, and waited for it. Hopper has a unit that
does the whole tile, called the Tensor Memory Accelerator, or TMA.

The setup happens once, on the host, before the kernel launches. A call to `cuTensorMapEncodeTiled`
builds a descriptor holding the element type, the base pointer, the shape of the matrix, the
distance between its rows, the shape of the tile you want, and how that tile should be scrambled on
arrival. Read that list again and notice what it is. It is a layout, in the sense Lecture 27 is about
to make precise, handed to hardware instead of to a loop.

Inside the kernel the copy is one call, made by one thread.

```cpp
__shared__ alignas(128) bf16 slot[BM * BK];   // 128 x 64 of bf16: 16,384 bytes
__shared__ barrier bar;

if (threadIdx.x == 0) init(&bar, blockDim.x); // all 128 threads will arrive
__syncthreads();

barrier::arrival_token token;
if (threadIdx.x == 0) {
    cp_async_bulk_tensor_2d_global_to_shared(slot, &tensor_map, col, row, bar);
    token = barrier_arrive_tx(bar, 1, sizeof(slot));   // 1 arrival, 16,384 bytes
} else {
    token = bar.arrive();                     // the others promise no bytes
}
bar.wait(std::move(token));                   // opens when both counts are full
```

The interesting object there is not the copy. It is `bar`. A `__syncthreads()` counts threads and
nothing else: it opens when everybody has arrived. This barrier counts two things, and the second one
is bytes. Thread 0 arms it with the size of the transfer, the copy engine reports its progress
against that number, and the wait opens only when every thread has arrived and every promised byte
has landed.

<text x="0" y="11.453" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="13px" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">open</text>

</svg>
  The barrier as a ledger with two columns. Thread 0 issues the copy and promises 16,384 bytes; the
  other 127 arrive promising nothing. The head count fills at once and the barrier stays shut,
  because the second column is the one the copy engine is still writing into.
</Figure>

Counting bytes rather than threads is what makes the copy free. The 127 threads that arrived and
promised nothing are not blocked on the transfer at all. They can be given a different job, and two
sections from now they are.

## The XOR that unties the banks

There is a reason the descriptor carries a scrambling mode, and it is the oldest problem in shared
memory.

Lecture 25 walked through the thirty-two doors: shared memory is 32 banks of 4 bytes, and when
the lanes of a warp ask for different addresses in the same bank the hardware serialises them. A tensor core sharpens the
problem, because it does not read a tile the way a loop does. It wants rows on one step and columns
on another, out of the same block of shared memory.

Work in 16-byte chunks, which is the unit a wide load moves, and think of a tile as an 8 by 8 grid of
them. Stored the obvious way, chunk $(r, c)$ lands in bank group $c$ for every row $r$. Reading a row
is then perfect: eight chunks, eight different bank groups, one turn. Reading a column is the worst
case the hardware has, because all eight chunks want bank group $c$ and get served one at a time, at
eight times the cost.

The fix is one exclusive-or. Store chunk $(r, c)$ at position $c \oplus r$ instead of $c$. Nothing
changes along a row, since exclusive-or with a fixed $r$ is a permutation of $0$ through $7$. And now
a column is $c \oplus 0, c \oplus 1, \ldots, c \oplus 7$, which is a permutation for the same reason.
Both directions are conflict free, out of one operation that costs nothing.

<Figure n="6">
  <svg slot="drawing" viewBox="0 0 647.9399999999999 438.25" width="648" role="img" aria-label="Two eight by eight grids of numbered chunks. On the left each cell carries its column number and column 3 is pink from top to bottom, and the bank strip beneath shows all eight chunks landing in bank 3. On the right each cell carries its column exclusive-or its row, column 3 reads 3, 2, 1, 0, 7, 6, 5, 4 in green, and the bank strip beneath shows one chunk in every bank." style="max-width:100%;height:auto;min-width:560px">

<g transform="translate(459.98 388) rotate(0 52.04999999999998 6.875)" data-s="3">
<text x="0" y="9.690999999999999" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="11px" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">bank 0 to bank 7</text>
</g>
<g transform="translate(440.01 412) rotate(0 74.025 8.125)" data-s="3">
<text x="0" y="11.453" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="13px" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">eight banks, one turn</text>
</g>
</svg>
  The same tile of 16-byte chunks, stored two ways, with column 3 marked. On the left every chunk of
  that column wants bank 3, and the eight of them are served one at a time. On the right the store
  position is the column exclusive-or the row, so the same eight chunks land in eight different
  banks and one turn serves them all. Row 0 is unchanged, because anything exclusive-or zero is
  itself.
</Figure>

The real thing works on address bits rather than on a toy grid. CuTe writes it `Swizzle<B, M, S>`,
which takes the $B$ address bits sitting $S$ places up and exclusive-ors them down into the $B$ bits
at position $M$. `Swizzle<3, 4, 3>` is the common choice for tensor core operands: three bits, so
eight positions, over chunks of 16 bytes, giving a pattern that repeats every 128 bytes. The pleasant
part is that you rarely write it. It is a field in the TMA descriptor, and the copy engine does the
scrambling as the bytes land.

## The factory

Every piece is now in place, and they assemble into a shape that has nothing to do with a loop.

The copy engine can run without a thread. The tensor cores can run without a thread. What remains is
to keep both busy at once, and the way to do that is to stop asking one warp-group to alternate
between them. Split the block's warp-groups by role. One of them does nothing but issue copies. The
others do nothing but multiply. This is called warp specialization, and PyTorch's write-up of the{' '}
<a href="https://pytorch.org/blog/cutlass-ping-pong-gemm-kernel/">CUTLASS ping-pong kernel</a>
{' '}states the split plainly: "each warp group takes on a specialized role of either Data producer
or Data consumer".

Between them sits a queue. Shared memory is carved into a small fixed number of slots, each big
enough for one pair of tiles, and the slots are used in a ring. Each slot carries its own pair of
barriers, and that pair is the entire synchronisation protocol:

- the producer waits on `empty[i]`, fills slot $i$, and signals `full[i]`;
- a consumer waits on `full[i]`, multiplies out of slot $i$, and signals `empty[i]`.

Nothing else is needed. No barrier across the block, no `__syncthreads()` around the loop, no thread
waiting on a thread. Each slot is a small contract between two groups that never look at each other.

<Figure n="7">
  <svg slot="drawing" viewBox="0 0 607.17 519.75" width="607" role="img" aria-label="Above, an orange producer warp-group box with an arrow into the third of five shared-memory slots, each slot carrying a full and an empty indicator, with the first two slots green and full, the third yellow and filling, the last two empty, and a dashed loop returning from slot 4 to slot 0. Two green consumer warp-group boxes take from the full slots. Below, two timelines: six orange load bars in a row and five green mma bars offset by one slot underneath them, with a marker where the first slot fills." style="max-width:100%;height:auto;min-width:600px">

<text x="0" y="9.690999999999999" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="11px" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">and from here nothing waits: every load runs under a multiply</text>

</svg>
  The factory. Above, one producer warp-group filling a ring of shared-memory slots, each slot
  holding its own pair of barriers, and two consumer warp-groups draining them into the tensor cores.
  Below, the same thing as a timeline: once the first slot is full, every load runs underneath a
  multiply and neither unit waits again. This is Lecture 26's double buffering with five buffers and
  nobody standing in the middle.
</Figure>

```cpp
if (warpgroup_id == 0) {                   // the producer
    setmaxnreg_dec();                      // it barely computes: give registers up
    for (int k = 0; k < k_tiles; ++k) {
        int slot = k % QSIZE;
        empty[slot].wait(phase(k));        // the consumer is done with this slot
        if (elected_one_thread) {
            tma_load(sA[slot], sB[slot], k);
            barrier_arrive_tx(full[slot], 1, BYTES_PER_SLOT);
        }
    }
} else {                                   // the consumers
    setmaxnreg_inc();                      // and they take what it gave up
    for (int k = 0; k < k_tiles; ++k) {
        int slot = k % QSIZE;
        full[slot].wait(phase(k));         // the bytes are in
        wgmma(d, sA[slot], sB[slot]);
        empty[slot].arrive();              // the slot may be refilled
    }
}
```

Two details in that skeleton pay for themselves. The first is `setmaxnreg`. A producer warp-group
computes almost nothing, so its threads need almost no registers, while each consumer thread is
holding 128 accumulator registers and would spill without more. The instruction moves the budget from
one group to the other at run time. PyTorch's post gives the sizes CUTLASS uses: the producer's count
goes down by 40 and each consumer's goes up by 232.

The second is `phase`. A barrier that is reused every `QSIZE` iterations cannot be waited on by name,
because a fast group would sail straight through a barrier the slow group opened one lap ago. Each
barrier carries a phase bit that flips every time it opens, and a waiter names the phase it expects.
Getting that wrong gives you a kernel that is right at one queue depth and silently wrong at another.

There are two ways to run the consumers and both are shipped. In the cooperative arrangement the two
consumer warp-groups work on the same output tile. In the ping-pong arrangement they work on
different ones, which lets one run its epilogue, the scaling and the store, while the other is still
multiplying. PyTorch gives the reason: "one can be using the tensor cores for MMA while the other
performs the epilogue, and then vice-versa".

## Keeping the line busy

The factory keeps one multiprocessor fed. Two things stop the whole chip from staying fed, and both
are about launch geometry rather than about the inner loop.

The first is waves. Lecture 26 measured the cliff: blocks are dealt to the multiprocessors in
rounds, and a launch that overshoots a round by one block pays a whole extra wave for it. The shape
this chapter has been building is no exception. A $4096 \times 4096$ output cut into
$128 \times 256$ tiles is $32 \times 16 = 512$ tiles, which is 3.88 waves on the 132
multiprocessors of an H100 SXM, so 4 waves, the last of which fills 116 of the 132 units. That is
97% of the machine used, and the missing 3% is worth having.

A persistent kernel takes it. Instead of launching one block per tile and letting the scheduler deal
them out, launch exactly as many blocks as there are multiprocessors, keep them alive for the whole
call, and have each one pull tiles from a shared counter until the work is gone. The wave boundary
disappears because there are no waves. Something better comes with it: a block about to start its
next tile can issue that tile's loads while the stores of the previous one are still in flight, so
the tail of one tile hides inside the head of the next. Pranjal's kernel goes from 631 to 660 TFLOP/s
in the step that makes the blocks persistent, hides the stores and groups the tiles for the cache.

## Scheduling is geometry

Which raises the question that decides the last stretch, and it is a question about drawing rather
than about code: in what order should the output tiles be visited?

Any order computes the same answer. The orders differ in how much the tiles in flight have in common.
A tile of $C$ at row $i$, column $j$ needs panel $i$ of $A$ and panel $j$ of $B$. If eight tiles are
in flight and they all sit in one row of the tile grid, they share one panel of $A$ and need eight
different panels of $B$, so nine in all. If they sit in a $4 \times 2$ block instead, they need four
and two. Same eight tiles, two-thirds of the operand traffic, and the L2 cache is what feels the
difference.

<g transform="translate(534.31 258) rotate(0 17.25999999999999 9.375)" data-s="2">
<text x="0" y="13.215" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="15px" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">6.67</text>
</g>
<g transform="translate(26 44) rotate(0 212.55 6.875)" data-s="0">
<text x="0" y="9.690999999999999" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="11px" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">yellow: the first eight tiles.  orange: the panels of A and B they need.</text>
</g>
<g transform="translate(69.36 284) rotate(0 284.5 7.5)" data-s="0">
<text x="0" y="10.572" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="12px" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">average panels of A and B that any eight tiles in flight need, over the whole schedule</text>
</g>
</svg>
  Three orders over the same 64 output tiles. The number under each is the average count of $A$ and
  $B$ panels that any eight consecutive tiles need, taken over the whole schedule. Row by row is the
  order a pair of nested loops gives you for free, and it is the worst of the three. The Hilbert
  curve is the best, because it never leaves a neighbourhood before it has finished with it.
</Figure>

The middle order is the one most libraries ship: walk a few rows of the tile grid at a time so that
consecutive tiles stay inside a compact block. It costs a few lines of index arithmetic and takes the
average from 9.86 panels to 7.02. A Hilbert curve is a space-filling curve that visits every cell of
a grid while keeping every prefix compact, and it reaches 6.67. On Pranjal's ladder the grouped order
is part of the step that gets to 660 TFLOP/s and lifts his L2 hit rate to 83%, against 70% for cuBLAS
on the same problem. The Hilbert curve is his last kernel and adds 6 TFLOP/s on top of 758.

Hopper adds one more level to that picture. Blocks can be grouped into a cluster, a set of blocks
guaranteed to be resident on nearby multiprocessors at the same time, and the members of a cluster
can read each other's shared memory directly. NVIDIA calls that distributed shared memory. It means a
panel of $B$ that two neighbouring blocks both want can be fetched once and multicast, instead of
fetched twice. A cluster behaves like a wider multiprocessor with a wider desk, and on Pranjal's
ladder it is worth 704 to 734 TFLOP/s, the step that first passes cuBLAS.

## The scoreboard

Here is the whole climb, on one H100 SXM, at $M = N = K = 4096$ in bf16. Every rate is Pranjal
Shankhdhar's, measured with CUDA 12.6 and averaged over eight runs. The percentages below and the
comparison against the speed of light are ours.

  <svg slot="drawing" viewBox="0 0 659.6899999999999 371" width="660" role="img" aria-label="Eleven green horizontal bars rising from 32 to 764 TFLOP/s, each labelled with the idea it adds, from Lecture 26 in bf16 through tensor cores and TMA, bigger tiles, the producer and consumer pipeline, persistent blocks, faster barriers, clusters and a Hilbert-curve order. Two dashed vertical lines mark cuBLAS at 716 and the speed of light at 989." style="max-width:100%;height:auto;min-width:560px">

</svg>
  Eleven kernels, one problem, one card. The first bar is Lecture 26's design ported to bf16. The
  dashed lines are NVIDIA's own library on the same problem and the speed of light from Figure 1.
  Every idea in this chapter is one bar.
</Figure>

Three things in that picture are worth saying out loud.

The first bar is 32 TFLOP/s, which is 3.2% of the machine. That is Lecture 26's design, the one that
reached 93.7% of cuBLAS on an RTX A6000, running on hardware whose fast path it does not know exists.
A kernel is only as good as the machine it was written for.

The second bar is 317, and it is one step: use the tensor cores and use the copy engine. Ten times
the speed, from adopting the two instructions this chapter is named after. Everything after that step
is about keeping those two busy, and everything after it adds up to another factor of 2.4.

The last bar is 764 against cuBLAS at 716. Divide and the kernel is 6.7% faster than NVIDIA's library
on this shape, which is what Pranjal reports, and he is careful to add that the margin moves with
size: about 2% at $N = 512$, 7 to 8% at 2048 and 4096, and 1.5% at 8192. It is also 77% of the 989
computed in Figure 1, and the missing 23% is where the honest answer lives. Part of it is the memory
system, part of it is the power limit from the warning above, and the rest is the last percent that
only exists below the language.

<Callout type="note">
  A CUDA kernel is compiled twice. The compiler emits PTX, a virtual instruction set that is stable
  across generations, which is why a binary built years ago still runs on a new card: if no machine
  code for that card is present, the driver compiles the PTX when the program launches. Then PTX is
  translated into SASS, the real instruction set of the real chip, which NVIDIA does not document.
  The last percent of a kernel lives at that boundary. Pranjal's seventh kernel goes from 660 to 704
  TFLOP/s, a jump of 6.7% by our arithmetic, from writing the barrier operations as PTX by hand
  rather than through the C++ interface. What is known publicly about SASS is known because people
  measured it: Zhe Jia, Marco Maggioni, Benjamin Staiger and Daniele Scarpazza's{' '}
  <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1804.06826">Dissecting the NVIDIA Volta GPU Architecture via Microbenchmarking</a>{' '}recovered
  the instruction encoding and the memory hierarchy of a whole generation by running experiments
  against it.
</Callout>

## Where this is going

Count what the thread lost in this chapter. It does not work out the address, because a descriptor
built on the host did that. It does not fetch, because a copy engine does. It does not multiply,
because a warp-group does. It does not even own the answer. What it owns is a role and a place in a
queue.

So what is a kernel written in, once it is not written in threads? Every piece of machinery above
turned out to be a promise about where numbers sit. The TMA descriptor is a shape, a stride, a tile
size and a scramble. The swizzle is a function from a coordinate to an address. The accumulator
fragment is a map from a thread and a register number to a place in a tile. A queue slot is a tile
shape with an offset.

Lecture 27 names that object. A layout is a function from coordinates to addresses, written as a
shape and a stride, and the operations a kernel needs become algebra on those functions. It is also
where four generations of this hardware get drawn as one timeline, each one taking another job away
from the thread, which is the story this chapter is a single frame of.

If you want to write these kernels rather than read about them, the{' '}
<a href="/resources">Library</a>{' '}
has the practice grounds: LeetGPU for CUDA judged on real hardware, Triton Puzzles for the
block-level way of thinking, and the GPU MODE reference kernels for problems people are still
competing on. Pranjal's{' '}
<a href="https://github.com/pranjalssh/fast.cu">fast.cu repository</a>
{' '}is the code behind every number in Figure 9.

<Callout type="check">
  1. An H100 PCIe has 114 multiprocessors and the same 4 tensor cores each at 1024 FLOP per clock. At
     the same 1.830 GHz, what is its speed of light? Its memory runs at 2 TB/s: where is its ridge
     point, and is a $128 \times 256$ tile any closer to it than on the SXM card?
  2. Take the wgmma shape m64n128k16. How many floating-point operations does one instruction do, how
     many accumulator registers does each of the 128 threads hold, and what fraction of a thread's
     255-register budget is that?
  3. In Figure 6, check that reading a row is still conflict free after the swizzle. Then find the one
     row the swizzle leaves exactly where it was, and say why it has to be that one.
  4. A $5000 \times 5000$ output is cut into $128 \times 256$ tiles and run on 132 multiprocessors,
     one block each. How many tiles, how many waves, and how many multiprocessors sit idle in the last
     wave? Compare the fraction of the machine used with the $4096 \times 4096$ case in the text.
  5. The producer warp-group in the factory listing does no arithmetic at all. With 128 threads to a
     warp-group and three warp-groups to a block, what fraction of the block's threads never touch a
     tensor core, and why is that a good trade rather than a waste?
  6. Redraw Figure 7 from memory, labels included. The drawing is the test.
</Callout>
